--- page 3 ---
India’s defence sector is being reshaped by a 
national commitment to design, develop and 
manufacture at home. Zen Technologies Limited has 
followed this path since 1993, building indigenous, 
IP-owned technologies for the training and 
readiness of the armed forces.
FY26 was a year of measured progress 
on that journey. The Company 
broadened from a defence training and 
simulation specialist into a diversified 
defence technology company serving 
India’s indigenous requirements across 
simulation, counter-drone systems, 
weapon platforms and robotics. This 
report sets out how that capability is 
taking shape and how it supports the 
country’s self-reliance in defence.

OWNED BY INDIA,

 ABOUT THE COMPANY
Zen Technologies Limited is one of India’s leading indigenous defence technology 
companies. Its purpose is to keep those who defend the nation ready for modern 
conflict. The Company designs, develops and manufactures its own technologies 
and retains ownership of the underlying intellectual property.
Founded in 1993 in Hyderabad, the 
Company began by building simulators 
for the Indian Army. Over three decades 
it has grown into a diversified defence 
technology company, listed on the BSE 
and the NSE, with capabilities across 
training and simulation, counter-drone 
systems, weapon platforms, combat 
robotics and naval simulation. This 
Vision
To be the global leader in defence training solutions 
and anti-drone technologies, empowering armed 
forces worldwide with advanced simulation and 
security systems.
Mission
To enhance the combat readiness of armed forces 
through innovative, cost-effective and sustainable 
training solutions, and to advance technology 
continuously, creating indigenous products that set 
new benchmarks in defence training and security.

shipped worldwide
evolution has been built on sustained, 
self-funded research and a consistent 
focus on indigenous ownership.
The Company’s technologies are 
used by the Indian armed forces, 
central and state police organisations 
and a growing number of customers 
abroad. Its counter-drone systems were 
deployed during Operation Sindoor , 
and its training systems are in service 
across the country. This combination 
of indigenous ownership and combat-
relevant technology has made the 
Company a trusted partner at home and 
a credible one in export markets across 
the Middle East, the CIS countries and 
South-East Asia.

THE TRIUMPH OF  
OPERATION SINDOOR
 THE TRIUMPH OF OPERATION SINDOOR
The Capability We Built
The Company’s Counter-UAS system was 
designed on a simple judgement, that a 
determined adversary would not confine its 
drones to the commercial frequency bands 
that most systems are built to jam. The system 
was therefore engineered to detect and jam a 
wide range of drone data links well beyond the 
commercial 2.4 GHz and 5.8 GHz spectrum.
• A multi-layer , multi-sensor architecture combining 
RF-based detection, radar and electro-optical sensors, 
capable of detecting even autonomous drones that fly 
without a link to a ground operator .
• Hawkeye, an AI-powered multi-sensor camera that lifts 
drone detection to 15 km, up from the earlier 7 to 8 km, 
with tracking accuracy of plus or minus 0.5 mrad.
• Hard-kill through in-service air defence guns, including 
the L -70, ZU-23 and Schilka, and AI-powered Remote 
Controlled Weapon Stations such as Barbarik 
and Parashu.
• VyomKavach, a hybrid Counter-UAS system combining 
AI, RF and rotary guns for swarm threats and high-
end UAVs.
How It Was Deployed
The system is rugged and 
highly mobile. It operated 
across the full range of Indian 
terrain, from the high-altitude 
posts of the northern and north-
eastern borders to the deserts 
of the western front. 
During the operation it 
detected up to 100 drone 
threats at a time with a response 
time of under 0.1 seconds, and 
it identified and neutralised 
hundreds of drones and swarm 
drones. As the adversary 
shifted to non-commercial 
frequencies, the wide-band 
design allowed the system to 
detect and defeat threats that 
conventional solutions missed.
During Operation Sindoor , the armed forces faced  
large-scale drone and swarm attacks across the western 
and northern fronts. The Company’s indigenous  
Counter-UAS system was among the equipment deployed, 
and it performed with distinction. This is the story of a 
technology built at home, proven in real combat.
Up to 100
Drone threats detected 
simultaneously
Under  
0.1 sec
Response time
15 KM
Hawkeye detection range,  
up from 7 to 8 km
What It Achieved
The weapons the Company 
fielded were 100% indigenous 
and the result of self-funded 
research. They helped blunt 
the drone-based threat and 
protected forces and assets 
across both fronts. The 
operation also validated the 
Company’s approach in the 
field and accelerated demand.

COMBAT PROVEN AND 
GLOBALLY READY
MESSAGE FROM THE CHAIRMAN AND MANAGING DIRECTOR
Dear Shareholders,
FY26 was a year of steady, deliberate 
progress for Zen Technologies Limited. 
Over the past two to three years we 
have broadened the Company from a 
leader in defence simulation to include 
counter drone  systems and allied 
technologies include counter drone  
systems and allied technologies . 
This experience has shaped our product 
roadmap. During the year we widened 
the frequency coverage of our anti-drone 
systems to stay ahead of adversaries 
who now operate at unconventional 
frequencies, strengthened our 
neutralisation options across RF and 
GNSS jamming, weapon stations and air 
defence gun integration, and developed 
an anti-drone simulator so that forces 
can rehearse against single drones and 
swarms in a realistic virtual environment.
A Broadening Product 
Portfolio
We launched a series of new platforms 
during the year and in the period that 
followed. Vrishabh, our unmanned 
ground vehicle, carries a payload of 150 
kilograms over a range of up to 100 
kilometres for combat, logistics and 
casualty evacuation, with indigenous 
content above 85%. Hyperstrike, 
our loitering strike platform, offers an 
affordable answer to the high-cost 
interceptor problem, with a speed of 
up to 400 kilometres per hour and a 
five-minute loiter-and-strike capability. 
Working with our associate AI Turing, 
we introduced a 30 mm programmable 
ammunition and a compact gun-and-
missile system for naval forces. Each of 
these products addresses a capability 
gap that current operational realities 
have brought into focus.
A defining feature of the year was the 
contribution of our subsidiaries and 
associates. Companies such as Applied 
Research International, Vector Technics, 
Bhairav Robotics and AI Turing added to 
consolidated revenue and earnings and 
validated the capital allocation decisions 
we have made over the years. Our 
role has evolved into that of a platform 
for indigenous defence technology, 
bringing together a group of specialist 
companies and giving them the support 
to meet the country’s requirements.
Recognition During the Year
Our work was recognised during the 
year with the CII Best Patent Portfolio 
Award 2025, and we achieved the 
highest Dun and Bradstreet rating of 
5A1, reflecting our financial strength to 
handle large government contracts. The 
Company also received a government 
licence under the Arms Act for the 
We can today offer the armed forces five 
capabilities, namely training simulation 
and systems, counter-drone solutions, 
automated weapon stations, combat 
robotics and drones. This has been built 
through sustained investment and a 
series of acquisitions and partnerships 
over several years, and FY26 was 
the year in which the breadth of that 
capability became clear .
The past year has reminded the world 
that preparedness is no longer a choice. 
Recent conflicts have changed the 
economics of warfare. When a low-cost 
drone can be met only by a far more 
expensive interceptor , the mathematics 
of defence breaks down. The lesson 
is clear . Layered anti-drone systems, 
combining software jamming with 
affordable hard-kill options, and well-
trained, combat-ready forces are now 
central to national security. These are the 
two areas in which we have built deep 
capability over three decades.
Operation Sindoor and 
Indigenous Capability
The clearest validation of our owned-by-
India philosophy came during Operation 
Sindoor . While conventional counter-
drone solutions often struggle against 
non-standard frequency bands, our 
Counter-UAS system was engineered 
on the expectation that sophisticated 
adversaries would operate outside 
commercial spectrums. It detected and 
neutralised hundreds of hostile drones 
and swarm drones, and supported the 
armed forces at a decisive moment. 
The operation confirmed that fully 
indigenous intellectual property, 
created for the specific requirements of 
Bharat, is a reliable shield for our forces.
manufacture of specified weapon 
systems, together with an ammunition 
licence, marking an important step 
in our evolution towards counter-
UAS technologies.
Financial Performance
FY26 presented a more measured profile 
than the exceptional growth of the 
previous year . Consolidated revenue was 
₹687.69 crore, compared with ₹973.64 
crore in FY25. Operational EBITDA was 
₹247.24 crore, at a margin of 35.95%, 
and reported EBITDA was ₹332.66 
crore. Profit after tax was ₹193.45 
crore after adjusting for non-controlling 
interests, against a reported profit after 
tax of ₹217.93 crore. These results 
should be read within the context of the 
defence sector’s procurement cycles. 
The strong revenue base of FY25 was 
driven by concentrated order execution, 
whereas FY26 saw order conversion 
timing extend beyond our expectations, 
with the bulk of recent orders scheduled 
for execution from FY27. Our long-term 
guidance of a 35% operational EBITDA 
margin and a 25% profit-after-tax margin 
remains intact.
A Strong Order Book
We closed the year with a consolidated 
order book of ₹1,336.04 crore, 
including new order inflows of ₹431.36 
crore secured during the fourth quarter 
alone. The standalone order book stood 
at ₹1,222.6 crore. The majority of the 
order book is scheduled for execution 
from FY27, and about ₹1,000 crore 
is expected to convert into revenue 
during that year . We maintained a strong 
balance sheet, with cash and bank 
balances of ₹1,308 crore and a debt-free 
position. We remain confident of our 
guidance of ₹4,000 crore of cumulative 
turnover across FY27 and FY28.
Looking Ahead
The global defence environment is 
undergoing structural change, and India’s 
continued emphasis on indigenisation 
and self-reliance provides a strong 
foundation for domestic companies 
focused on innovation and IP ownership. 
For innovators such as the Zen, the draft 
Defence Acquisition Procedure 2026 
is a significant document. We expect 
it to compress the procurement cycle 
considerably and to open new growth 
opportunities for companies that have 
built genuine indigenous capability and 
intellectual property. We also expect 
order wins to begin flowing through in 
the near term.
On behalf of the Board, I thank our 
armed forces, customers, shareholders, 
employees and partners for their 
continued trust. Their confidence inspires 
us to keep building technologies that 
contribute to India’s long-term defence 
preparedness and security.
Swadeshi is not only 
manufacturing. It 
is design, develop 
and manufacture 
in Bharat. Bharat 
should aim to be the 
research laboratory 
of the world, not the 
factory of the world.
Warm regards,
Ashok Atluri
CHAIRMAN AND 
MANAGING DIRECTOR
Zen Technologies Limited

ADVANCING DEFENCE  
PREPAREDNESS 
THROUGH 
TECHNOLOGY
PRODUCT PORTFOLIO
The evolving character of modern warfare has increased the importance 
of technologies that strengthen operational preparedness, situational 
awareness and training effectiveness. Simulation-based training, integrated 
combat environments and counter-unmanned aerial technologies are now 
central to defence preparedness.
Live Simulation
Armour Combat Training System  
(Zen ACTS TM), a programmable  
electro-mechanical system for versatile 
targets, for outdoor and indoor ranges.
TACSIM® Tactical Engagement 
Simulator, which trains soldiers in field 
craft, battle drills and live-fire practice, 
and suits commando units, special 
forces, training centres and  
law-enforcement agencies.
Training Solutions  
and Live Ranges
Our live ranges bridge the firing 
range and the battlefield, offering 
instrumented environments for 
precision marksmanship and 
tactical training. Troops practise 
marksmanship, tactical situations 
and missions with live ammunition 
under conditions close to  
real-world scenarios. 
Containerised Tubular Shooting Range 
(CTSR), a compact, secure indoor 
alternative to live firing that can be 
extended with additional containers.
Containerised Indoor Shooting Range 
(CISR), a self-contained, transportable 
live-fire facility built with acoustic and 
ricochet materials.
Containerised Small Arms Firing Range 
(CSAFR), a modular live-fire range 
offering realism, zero danger area and 
rapid deployment for military, police 
and paramilitary use.
Shoot House, a secure indoor 
facility for training security 
personnel to neutralise threats in a 
closed environment.
Air-to-Ground Firing Range Scoring 
System, which deploys targets, 
tracks their movement and records 
hit-and-miss scores for air-to-ground 
weapons delivery.
Smart Target System (Zen STS®) with 
LOMAH TM, an electro-mechanical, 
software-driven acoustical projectile 
detection and reporting system for day 
and night firing.
Multi-Functional Target System 
(MFTS®), a portable, programmable 
system for versatile targets, for use at 
outdoor and indoor ranges.
Tank Targets, static and moving tank 
target systems for range and tactical 
firing practice.

PRODUCT PORTFOLIO (CONTD.)
Simulators for  
Virtual Training
Virtual simulation is a key offering, 
providing controlled, cost-
effective training that allows many 
personnel to train simultaneously 
without compromising quality.
Armoured  
Vehicle Simulators
Tank Driving Simulator , a 
containerised simulator offering 
progressive training for T-90 tank 
operations across diverse terrains.
Tank Crew Gunnery Simulator , a 
gunnery training simulator for T-90 
tank gunner trainees.
IFV / APC Driving Simulator , a high-
fidelity simulator with six-degrees-
of-freedom motion, improving 
safety and reducing cost.
IFV / APC Crew Gunnery Simulator , 
which replicates actual controls 
to improve weapon handling 
and targeting for gunner and 
commander training.
Medium Machine Gun Simulator 
(MMG SIM), an interactive indoor 
aid for engagement techniques, fire 
control and tactical employment.
Automatic Grenade Launcher Simulator 
(AGL SIM), a complete training solution 
for automatic grenade launcher crews.
81mm Mortar Integrated Simulator, 
which builds proficiency in operating 
and firing the 81mm mortar.
Anti-Tank Guided Missile Simulator 
(ATGM SIM), which trains operators in 
handling and firing missiles, with real-
time performance tracking.
Infantry Combat Vehicle Driving 
Simulator (BMP II DS), which trains 
recruits and drivers to operate the  
ICV or APC.
Artillery Forward Observers’ Simulator 
(ARTYFOS), which trains observers 
who direct long-range, indirect fire.
Anti-Aircraft Air Defence Simulator 
(3ADS), which trains operators on 
laying procedures in simulated and 
field conditions.
Integrated Air Defence Combat 
Simulator (Zen IADCS), which trains 
L-70 and ZU-23-2 gun crews with 
realistic weapon handling and 
battle scenarios.
BMP II Integrated Missile Simulator 
(BMP II IMS), which trains gunners and 
commanders in handling and firing 
different weapon systems.
Advanced Weapons Simulator 
(AWESIM®), which recreates a small-
arms firing range indoors for recruits, 
special forces and commandos.
Infantry Weapons Training Simulator 
(IWTS®), a modular wireless system 
for marksmanship and battle handling 
of weapons, individually and up to 
a section.
Combat Weapons Training Simulator 
(CWTS), an indoor simulator 
that enhances firing skills across 
infantry weapons with future-ready 
upgrade capability.
Vehicle Driving 
Simulators
Driving Training Simulator (Zen DTS®), 
a versatile indoor system for light, 
medium and heavy vehicle drivers, with 
a to-scale cabin and actual controls.
Driver Aptitude Testing System  
(Zen DATS), which tests alertness, 
depth perception, peripheral and night 
vision, and recovery from glare.
Combat Training 
Centres
Our Combat Training Centres 
bring together live, virtual, 
constructive and mixed-
reality systems to develop and 
maintain combat-ready soldiers.
They can simulate offence, 
defence and stability 
operations, and support joint 
power projection, ground 
manoeuvre, air operations, 
fire support and logistics. The 
centres feature instrumented 
range areas, simulation facilities 
and a dedicated opposing force 
to provide a capable adversary 
for training.

ENGINEERING THE    
UNASSAILABLE
INNOVATION
At the Company, research and 
development is a core part of 
our identity. Our owned-by-India 
philosophy holds that the design of a 
product is a national strategic asset, 
and we work to keep ownership of 
that design in India.
The Science of Savings
To establish the environmental signatures 
of its simulators scientifically, the 
Company commissioned TERI, The 
Energy and Resources Institute, in 
March 2023 to carry out a joint study. 
The first study was launched on World 
Environment Day, 5 June 2023, and 
was well received. A second, deeper 
study has since been completed 
and is being prepared for launch. It 
examines a larger set of simulator types, 
assessing emissions, the net present 
value of environmental savings and the 
single-day cost of non-induction. NITI 
Aayog has also featured the subject of 
green training.
A Record of Self-
Funded Innovation
The Company funds its research from 
its own resources and has invested 
consistently in developing indigenous 
technologies over many years. Its 
units hold accreditation from the 
Department of Scientific and Industrial 
Research (DSIR), Ministry of Science 
and Technology. Detailed R&D-intensity 
metrics are provided in the Key 
Performance Indicators section.
Expanding Our 
Innovation Footprint
The Company is expanding 
its research footprint to meet 
demand for autonomous and 
aerial defence solutions. A new 
R&D and manufacturing facility is 
being developed at the Electronic 
Manufacturing Cluster in Tuem, Goa, 
and the Company continues to build 
its software and AI development 
capability for combat robotics and 
autonomous systems.

INNOVATION (CONTD.)
Emerging Autonomous 
and Intelligent 
Defence Platforms
The future battlefield is being reshaped 
by autonomy, artificial intelligence, 
software-defined systems and rapid-
response technologies. The Company 
is expanding its capabilities across 
autonomous robotics, advanced 
aerial systems and cyber-secure 
operational architectures.
Hyperstrike Loitering 
Platform
Hyperstrike is a loitering strike platform 
with a speed of up to 400 km per hour 
and a five-minute loiter-and-strike 
capability over a range beyond 20 km. 
It combines AI-enabled tracking and 
guidance with a manual override, carries 
a proximity payload of about 200 g on a 
4 kg airframe, and is designed for scaled 
production. Its high-speed propulsion 
system is supplied by Vector Technics.
Cyber Security Suite
As warfare extends into cyber and 
network-centric domains, the Company 
is strengthening its defence-grade 
cyber security capabilities. Developed 
with our subsidiaries, the suite is an 
integrated solution that can be installed 
at command and control centres, on 
ships and in aircraft. It is built around 
six layers, namely awareness and 
training, monitoring and intelligence, 
protection and prevention, encryption 
and data sovereignty, threat perception 
and response, and post-quantum 
readiness, and is designed with zero 
foreign dependencies.
Vrishabh Unmanned 
Ground Vehicle
Vrishabh is an unmanned ground vehicle 
supporting autonomous mobility, tactical 
logistics and remote deployment. It has a 
payload of 150 kg, a speed of up to 50 km 
per hour and a range of up to 100 km, and 
has been integrated with weapon systems 
including MMG and LMG. Indigenous 
content exceeds 85%, and commercial 
launch is planned for FY27.

THE FOUNDATION OF ATMANIRBHAR 
DEFENCE INNOVATION
OUR INFRASTRUCTURE
The Company’s infrastructure 
reflects a long-standing 
commitment to indigenous 
research and technology 
development, aligned with 
India’s Atmanirbhar Bharat 
vision. Its facilities support the 
complete lifecycle of defence 
technology development, from 
concept design to testing, 
integration and manufacturing.
 1
3 2
The following facilities support the Company’ s research, demonstration and 
manufacturing activities.

participant in India’s evolving defence

POWERING THE FUTURE OF 
INDIGENOUS DEFENCE 
TECHNOLOGIES
STRATEGIC FOCUS AREAS
Our mission is to build responsible readiness, ensuring that 
those who protect our nation are equipped with advanced, 
reliable and intelligent tools. We continue to invest in our 
core competencies of indigenous research, human capital, 
scalable infrastructure and global partnerships.
Strengthening Research 
and Development 
Capabilities
We maintain a continuous cycle 
of innovation, with a current 
focus on AI-enabled simulation 
environments, autonomous 
detection and intelligent decision-
support systems. During the year 
we strengthened our AI-enabled 
Remote Controlled Weapon 
Stations, which deliver precision 
fire without exposing soldiers, 
and our AI-powered Return Fire 
Module, which enables remote 
pose estimation and target gesture 
recognition in real time.
Enhancing  
Export Potential
The combat-proven status of our 
systems has increased interest 
from international markets. We are 
expanding our footprint across 
the Middle East, the CIS countries 
and South-East Asia, and our 
partnership with AVT Simulation 
supports entry into the United 
States defence market.
Advancing  
Counter-Drone 
Solutions
Operation Sindoor reinforced  
the importance of layered  
counter-drone systems. The 
Company has developed Hawkeye, 
an AI-powered multi-sensor camera 
with detection ranges from 8 km to 
15 km. Our hybrid C-UAS integrates 
AI, RF and rotary machine guns to 
neutralise swarming drone threats.
Expanding the Product 
Portfolio and Scope
We are expanding across land, air 
and maritime domains, diversifying 
beyond land simulation into 
autonomous robotics, aerospace 
components, naval simulation 
and UAV platforms. Our Zen FIST 
(Futuristic Integrated Solution 
for Training) digitally integrates 
tank driving and crew gunnery 
simulators into a single tactical 
entity. Prahasta marks our entry into 
high-risk mine-clearing and counter-
insurgency operations.
Capitalising on 
Regulatory Tailwinds
The Company is well positioned to 
benefit from Make in India, positive 
indigenisation lists and the growing 
policy focus on IDDM technologies. 
During the year the Company 
achieved the highest Dun and 
Bradstreet rating of 5A1, reflecting 
its financial strength to handle large-
scale government contracts.
Integrating Strategic 
Acquisitions
The Company integrates 
new capabilities to compress 
development timelines. The 
acquisition of Applied Research 
International and its group entities 
has expanded our presence in 
marine and offshore simulation, 
while investments in Vector 
Technics and Bhairav Robotics 
have strengthened our capabilities 
in aerospace propulsion and 
autonomous robotics.
Leveraging Intellectual 
Property
Our business model is built on 
the belief that India must own its 
intellectual property rather than 
assemble technology under licence. 
We safeguard our position by 
creating high entry barriers through 
a growing patent portfolio. During 
the year we secured patents for the 
Tank Containerised Crew Gunnery 
Simulator and the Infantry Virtual 
Training Simulation System.
Focusing on Green 
Training and Simulation
Our simulation-based training 
reduces ammunition consumption, 
fuel use and environmental 
impact while maintaining training 
effectiveness. An independent 
study by TERI found that 15% 
penetration of our Infantry Weapon 
Training Simulator could save the 
armed forces ₹461.20 crore each 
year . During the year the Company 
secured a contract for India’s first 
Combat Training Node at the 
Infantry School, Mhow.
Pursuing Partnerships
Our strategy to build smart with the 
right partners supports our move to 
the next technological threshold. 
During the year the Company 
entered a memorandum of 
understanding with Italy-based TXT 
Group for advanced pilot training 
solutions, from part-task trainers to 
full-flight simulators.

ADVANCING THE PARADIGM   
OF GREEN TRAINING
SUSTAINABILITY
The Science of Savings
To establish the environmental 
signatures of its simulators scientifically, 
the Company commissioned TERI, 
The Energy and Resources Institute, in 
March 2023 to carry out a joint study. 
The first study was launched on World 
Environment Day, 5 June 2023, and 
was well received. A second, deeper 
study has since been completed and is 
being prepared for launch. It examines 
a larger set of simulator types, 
assessing emissions, the net present 
value of environmental savings and the 
single-day cost of non-induction. NITI 
Aayog has also featured the subject of 
green training.
Building a Centre of Excellence
The Company is establishing a Centre of Excellence 
for Green Training and Environmental Sustainability. 
The platform will support specialised education 
on sustainable defence practices, technological 
advancement to refine simulation realism, and policy 
research as a collaborative forum on green technology 
in the defence sector .
This spread reflects the Company’s Green Training programme. Zen has been 
at the forefront of spreading awareness on Green Training, an approach that 
reduces the environmental cost of military training. 
Training that does not pollute the atmosphere is called green training, and 
simulator-based training is inherently green because it dwarfs the need to 
fire large quantities of live ammunition, the biggest single polluter in field 
exercises, while also cutting fuel use and greenhouse gas emissions.

Evidence-led 
Environmental Gains
The studies show that even a small 
shift of field-mode training to 
simulation delivers environmental 
and economic gains worth hundreds 
of crores. 
A representative finding is that a ₹15 
crore investment in simulators saves 
₹380 crore over 30 years, a return 
of about 2,500%. Simulator-based 
training also delivers significant 
savings in greenhouse gas emissions 
and reduces the consumption of fuel 
and live ammunition.
Recognised on a 
Global Index
The Company’s sustainability framework 
is externally benchmarked. In the 
S&P Global Corporate Sustainability 
Assessment for FY 2024-25, which 
underlies the Dow Jones Sustainability 
Index, Zen recorded an ESG score of 67, 
with 61 in Environmental, 72 in Social 
and 69 in Governance & Economic. 
The assessment measures performance 
against industry-specific criteria and 
against DJSI constituents, placing the 
Green Training programme within a 
verified global benchmark.

MANAGEMENT DISCUSSION 
AND ANALYSIS
MANAGEMENT DISCUSSION AND ANALYSIS
Global Economic Review
The global economy remained steady in 2025 despite 
elevated geopolitical uncertainty and fragmented trade 
dynamics. Global GDP growth is estimated at approximately 
3.4% in 2025, supported by investment in artificial intelligence 
and technology infrastructure and by easing financial 
conditions across major economies. Advanced economies 
grew by about 1.9%, with the United States at 2.1% and the 
euro area at about 1.4%. Emerging markets continued to lead, 
with developing Asian economies growing by about 4.4%.
The outlook became more fragile towards early 2026 following 
escalation in the Middle East and continued disruption linked 
to the Russia-Ukraine conflict. The IMF projects world growth 
of 3.1% in 2026. Global inflation moderated to 4.1% in 2025 
as supply-chain conditions improved, although energy-price 
and commodity risks remain elevated.
Source: IMF World Economic Outlook, April 2026.
The Era of Multipolar Conflict
The geopolitical environment reflected a continued shift 
towards a fragmented, multipolar world order . Ongoing 
conflicts in Eastern Europe and the Middle East, rising 
Indo-Pacific tensions and strategic competition around 
critical technologies significantly altered security priorities. 
In 2025, global military expenditure reached approximately 
US$2.63 trillion, up from US$2.48 trillion in 2024, driven by 
increases in Europe and the Middle East. The United States 
spent about US$954 billion on defence in 2025. Europe’s 
contribution to global military expenditure rose above 22%, 
from 17% in 2022, with Germany a primary catalyst. The 
global defence-to-GDP ratio rose from 1.89% to 2.01%.
Source: SIPRI, 2026; IISS, The Military Balance 2026.
Catalysts for Defence 
Transformation
GEOPOLITICAL TENSIONS:
Countries are moving beyond stockpile 
replenishment towards comprehensive 
modernisation focused on agility, rapid 
response and technological superiority.
INDUSTRIAL SOVEREIGNTY:
Procurement is shifting from localised assembly 
towards technological independence, with nations 
prioritising sovereign IP so that critical systems are 
fully owned.
NATO SPEND:
At the 2025 Hague Summit, NATO allies committed 
to invest 5% of GDP a year in defence by 2035, of 
which 1.5% will be allocated to critical infrastructure 
and industrial base.
TECHNOLOGICAL ADVANCEMENT:
Spending is increasingly driven by asymmetric 
advantages, where low-cost autonomous drones 
and AI-integrated networks challenge high-value 
assets, raising research intensity and the priority on 
sovereign IP .
Source: IISS, The Military Balance 2026; World Economic Forum, 
Global Risks Report 2026.
Global Defence 
Trends and Outlook
ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE AND AUTONOMY:
Military-grade autonomy has become operational 
across unmanned aerial, ground and naval 
vehicles, and AI is embedded in command and 
surveillance systems.
THE ELECTROMAGNETIC AND 
CYBER FRONTIER:
Digital infrastructure is now a primary frontline 
concern, with rising investment to guard against 
electronic hijacking and spoofing.
GREEN DEFENCE AND 
RESOURCE OPTIMIZATION:
Militaries are aligning security goals with 
sustainability, with reduced fuel dependency 
also simplifying logistics.
IMMERSIVE TRAINING AND 
COMBAT READINESS:
Augmented and virtual reality tools simulate 
complex, multi-domain environments that 
are difficult to replicate in live training, 
improving performance while reducing cost.
AUTONOMOUS LOGISTICS AND 
PREDICTIVE MAINTENANCE:
 Automation of the military supply chain keeps front-
line assets operational with minimal downtime.
Source: World Economic Forum, Global Risks Report 2026.

MANAGEMENT DISCUSSION AND ANALYSIS (CONTD.)
Indian Economic Review
India remained the fastest-growing major economy for 
the fourth consecutive year , with real GDP growth of 
7.4%, supported by strong domestic fundamentals and 
sustained investment. Growth was broad-based, supported 
by continued government capital expenditure on roads, 
railways, defence and logistics, rapid digitalisation, the 
Production Linked Incentive scheme supporting Make 
in India, strength in IT , financial services and tourism, and 
resilient domestic consumption following income-tax and 
GST relief.
Source: Ministry of Finance, Government of India, 2026.
The Indian Defence Industry
The Indian defence industry has undergone a paradigm 
shift, moving from a reactive procurement model towards 
a high-velocity, indigenous manufacturing base. Sustained 
policy support, rising private participation and a growing 
emphasis on sovereign intellectual property have combined 
to create one of the most supportive environments the sector 
has seen. For a company built on indigenous research and 
IP ownership, these tailwinds are directly relevant to the 
opportunity ahead.
Record Production and Exports
Indigenous defence production reached a historic high of 
approximately ₹1.54 lakh crore in FY25, and production in 
FY26 is projected to surpass the ₹1.75 lakh crore target. 
Defence exports for FY25 stood at a record ₹23,622 crore, 
with Indian-made systems reaching over 100 nations, 
including the United States and France. Exports are reported 
to have reached an all-time high of approximately ₹38,424 
crore in FY26, an increase of about 62.66%, reaching over 
80 countries.
Source: Press Information Bureau and Ministry of Defence, Government of 
India, 2026.
The Year of Reforms and DAP 2026
The Ministry of Defence designated 2025 the Year of 
Reforms, leading to the release of the draft Defence 
Acquisition Procedure 2026. The new framework introduces 
Long-Term Bulk Acquisition, giving industry ten-year visibility 
on orders, and Low-Cost Capital Acquisition for fast-moving 
technologies such as drones and artificial intelligence. The 
draft places clear emphasis on indigenously designed, 
developed and manufactured technologies, an approach 
that rewards companies that self-fund genuine capability 
development rather than assemble technology under licence.
Source: Press Information Bureau, Government of India, 2026.
Streamlining Readiness through DPM 
2025
Launched in October 2025, the Defence Procurement 
Manual 2025 overhauled revenue procurement worth 
approximately ₹1 lakh crore, simplifying procedures for 
spares and services so that the armed forces can maintain 
peak operational readiness with domestic support.
Source: Ministry of Defence, Government of India, 2025.
The Rise of the Private Defence Sector
The private sector's contribution to defence production 
has climbed to 23%, and order books for private firms are 
estimated to reach ₹55,000 crore by the end of FY26. 
Initiatives such as the ADITI scheme provide grants of up 
to ₹25 crore to start-ups for deep-tech innovation in areas 
including artificial intelligence, quantum technology and 
anti-drone systems. Beyond the established corridors in 
Uttar Pradesh and Tamil Nadu, new clusters in Maharashtra 
are operationalising, integrating over 16,000 MSMEs into 
the defence supply chain.
Source: Press Information Bureau, Government of India, 2026.
Budget Allocations and a Strong 
Indigenisation Push
The Union Budget for FY27 reinforced the Government 
of India's commitment to defence modernisation, 
indigenous capability development and long-term military 
preparedness, with a record allocation of approximately 
₹7.85 lakh crore to the Ministry of Defence, an increase of 
about 15.19% over the previous year . Of this, ₹2.19 lakh 
crore was earmarked under the capital head for defence 
services, a growth of 21.84%, with approximately ₹1.85 
lakh crore towards capital acquisitions of advanced 
platforms, weapon systems, drones and counter-drone 
technologies. About 75% of the capital acquisition budget 
was earmarked for domestic defence firms, reinforcing the 
policy emphasis on indigenisation. India's counter-drone 
market is projected to grow at a 24.4% compound annual 
rate to reach US$1.39 billion by 2030.
Source: Union Budget FY27, Government of India; MarketsandMarkets, 2025.
Key Trends in the Indian Defence Sector
NETWORKED AND DATA-CENTRIC WARFARE:
The Indian Army has declared 2026 the year of 
Networking and Data Centricity, focused on 
connecting every soldier , sensor and weapon 
platform into a unified C4ISR framework for real-
time information dominance.
UBIQUITY OF AUTONOMOUS SYSTEMS 
AND ROBOTICS:
Autonomy is becoming a standard 
capability, reflected in the vision of an eagle 
on the arm of every soldier and the setting 
up of 19 drone training centres and hubs.
COUNTER-UAS DEMAND:
In the wake of regional conflicts, demand for 
layered anti-drone systems has surged, with a shift 
towards wide-band electronic warfare solutions 
that can disrupt adversarial communication and 
navigation across non-standard frequencies.
LIVE, VIRTUAL AND 
CONSTRUCTIVE TRAINING:
The military is moving towards Integrated Combat 
Training Nodes that combine live firing with virtual 
and constructive simulation for high-fidelity, 
force-on-force exercises while reducing wear on 
physical assets.
A SOVEREIGN AI ROADMAP:
The Army's AI roadmap integrates 
artificial intelligence across intelligence, 
surveillance and reconnaissance, weapon 
platforms and autonomous systems, 
shortening the sensor-to-shooter loop.
AI-DRIVEN PERSONALISATION 
AND ANAL YTICS:
Modern simulators use artificial intelligence 
and big data to provide millisecond-level 
feedback, adjusting each soldier's training to 
their performance.
MILITARISATION OF SPACE:
With Mission Sudarshan Chakra, there is 
intensified focus on space-based surveillance 
and secure satellite communication.
LA YERED COUNTER-DRONE DOMINANCE:
As drones become low-cost strike assets, 
India's counter-drone market is surging, with a 
shift towards Integrated Drone Detection and 
Interdiction Systems that combine soft-kill wide-
band RF jamming and cyber takeover with hard-kill 
lasers and kinetic interceptors.

Company Overview
Established in 1993, Zen Technologies Limited has grown 
from a specialised simulation firm into a diversified defence 
company. Headquartered in Hyderabad, with a presence 
across India, the UAE and the United States, the Company 
delivers high-fidelity combat environments and mission-
critical defence hardware. Its operations are driven by three 
offerings, namely training solutions including live ranges and 
virtual simulation, counter-drone detection and neutralisation 
technologies, and advanced military hardware including 
robotic surveillance systems and remote weapon stations. 
The Company holds CMMI Maturity Level 5 and ISO/
IEC 27001:2022 certifications. As at 31 March 2026 its 
consolidated order book stood at approximately ₹1,336 crore.
Financial Performance
The Company achieved revenue of ₹687.69 crore in FY26, 
compared with ₹973.64 crore in FY25. The moderation was 
largely attributable to delays in order finalisation, shifting 
procurement priorities and the milestone-based nature of 
defence contracts. Underlying momentum remained robust 
across simulation systems, counter-UAS technologies and 
emerging autonomous platforms.
The Company maintained strong profitability, supported by a 
differentiated technology portfolio and operational discipline. 
Reported EBITDA was ₹332.66 crore, at a margin of 48.37%, 
and operational EBITDA, which excludes other operating 
income, was ₹247.24 crore, at a margin of 35.95%. Profit 
after tax was ₹193.45 crore after adjusting for non-controlling 
interests, against a reported profit after tax of ₹217.93 crore. 
The Company maintained cash and bank balances of over 
₹1,308 crore and a debt-free position. Its consolidated order 
book as at 31 March 2026 stood at approximately ₹1,336 
crore, providing strong revenue visibility.

Acquisitions during the Year
During FY26 the Company continued to build its integrated defence technology ecosystem. In June 2025 it acquired a majority stake 
in TISA Aerospace, strengthening its presence in loitering munitions, offensive UAV platforms and autonomous aerial systems. During 
the year the Company also completed the acquisition of the remaining 24% stake in Applied Research International Private Limited, 
making it a wholly owned subsidiary and strengthening its position in naval, marine and offshore simulation. In November 2025 the 
Company acquired a 76% equity stake in Anawave Systems & Solutions Private Limited, adding scenario-based naval training systems 
designed to integrate with existing naval command and control architecture. The Company continued to integrate investments made 
during the previous year , including Vector Technics, Bhairav Robotics and the ARI group entities.
Risks and Mitigation
The Company operates at the intersection of national security and rapid technological change. Its risk-management framework is 
designed to convert potential vulnerabilities into competitive strengths.
Risk Mitigation
Competitive dynamics:
Global players may use aggressive pricing to secure large 
contracts.
We focus on deep product customisation, superior technical 
performance and long-term service relationships that 
provide value beyond the initial purchase price.
Macroeconomic volatility:
Slowdowns or fiscal tightening can reduce budgets and extend 
procurement timelines.
A diversified portfolio and a growing geographic footprint 
reduce our reliance on any single economy or budget cycle.
Order-book uncertainty:
Developing bespoke systems without finalised orders can 
pressure cash flows.
We prioritise milestone-based agreements and maintain 
close collaboration with clients throughout design and 
development.
Procurement structure:
Tender processes often favour the lowest bidder , which may not 
reflect lifecycle value.
We focus on cost optimisation and lean manufacturing while 
delivering the technological value required for mission 
success.
Supply-chain fragility:
Geopolitical instability and component shortages can delay 
production.
We maintain a resilient, multi-tiered supplier network and 
robust contingency planning.
Technological obsolescence:
Rapid innovation carries the risk of systems becoming outdated.
We invest continuously in next-generation research, agile 
development and strategic partnerships.
Cyber security and data integrity:
A threat-sensitive environment makes the Company a target for 
digital incursion and IP theft.
We adhere to ISO/IEC 27001:2022 and conduct regular 
audits and staff training.
Human capital and talent retention:
Specialised talent is in high global demand.
We foster a mission-driven culture, clear growth pathways 
and rigorous succession planning.
Human Resources and Industrial 
Relations
The Company regards its people as the primary architects 
of its success. In FY26 it accelerated investment in technical 
upskilling and leadership development, strengthening its 
capability base in a competitive market for specialised talent. 
As at 31 March 2026 its multidisciplinary team had grown to 
421 professionals. The Company prioritises transparent and 
constructive engagement with all stakeholders.
Internal Control Systems and 
Their Adequacy
The Company has established an internal control framework 
matched to the scale and complexity of its operations, 
providing a foundation for financial integrity, operational 
accountability and regulatory compliance. The framework 
rests on a structured governance hierarchy with clearly 
defined roles and reporting lines, documented policies and 
standard operating procedures, quarterly internal audits by an 
independent audit function, and direct oversight by the Audit 
Committee of the Board, which monitors the resolution of audit 
findings each quarter .
Cautionary Statement
This report contains forward-looking statements based on 
current expectations, assumptions and projections about future 
events. These statements involve risks and uncertainties that 
could cause actual results to differ materially. The Company is 
under no obligation to update any forward-looking statement 
publicly, whether as a result of new information, future 
developments or otherwise. External factors, such as global 
economic conditions, regulatory changes or technological 
disruption, may affect performance in ways that cannot 
be predicted.
MANAGEMENT DISCUSSION AND ANALYSIS (CONTD.)
(a)  The Debt-Equity Ratio declined by 94.44% on account of repayment of borrowings during the year.
(b)  DSCR improved by 409.80% due to reduction in debt service obligations following repayment of borrowings.
(c)  Lower PAT in FY26 due to moderation in  revenue order execution timing; equity base has expanded.
(d)  The Inventory Turnover Ratio declined by 48.61% due to lower cost of goods sold in line with the decrease in revenue, while inventory levels remained at similar levels.
(e)  Lower trade receivable due to lower revenue base during the year.
(f)  EBIT reduced in line with lower revenue; capital employed grew on account of strong equity accretion.

IEPF RELATED INFORMATION:
19. Pursuant t o the provisions of Sections 124 and 125 
of the Act and the Investor Education and Protection 
Fund Authority (Accounting, Audit, Transfer and Refund) 
Rules, 2016 (“IEPF Rules”) dividends that remain unpaid 
or unclaimed for a period of seven year or more, are 
mandatorily required to be transferred to the Investor 
Education and Protection Fund (“IEPF”). Accordingly, 
unpaid or unclaimed dividends up to the financial year 
2017-18 have been transferred to the IEPF .
 As p er the provisions of Section 124(6) of the Act read 
with the IEPF Rules as amended, all shares in respect of 
which dividends have remained unclaimed for seven 
consecutive years or more for the financial year ended 
31st March, 2018 were transferred by the Company to the 
IEPF . The Company had sent individual communications to 
the concerned shareholders whose shares were liable to 
be transferred to the IEPF .
20. The unclaimed equity dividend for the year ended March 
31, 2019, will be transferred in the month of November , 
2026, to the “Investor Education and Protection Fund 
(IEFP)” on expiry of 7 years from the date of transfer to the 
Unpaid Dividend Account, pursuant to Section 124 of the 
Companies Act, 2013 (Section 205A of the Companies 
Act, 1956). Members who have not encashed their 
dividend warrants for the said financial year or subsequent 
year(s) are requested to send the same to the Company or 
its Registrars and Share Transfer Agents (“RTA”) for issue of 
fresh demand drafts.
21. As p er the provisions of Section 125 of the Act and the 
IEPF Rules, members whose unclaimed dividend and 
equity shares have been transferred to IEPF , may claim the 
refund by making an application to the IEPF Authority in 
web form IEPF-5 available on www.mca.gov.in.

Item No.5: Ratification of remuneration payable 
to Cost Auditors for the financial year 2026-27:
Pursuant to Section 148 of the Companies Act, 2013 read 
with the Companies (Cost Records and Audit) Rules, 2014, as 
amended from time to time, the Company is required to have 
the audit of its cost records conducted by a cost accountant in 
practice.
The Board, on the recommendation of the Audit Committee, at 
its meeting held on May 01, 2026, approved the appointment 
and remuneration payable to the cost auditors, M/ s. M P R 
& Associates, Cost Accountants (Firm Reg. No: 000413), 
Hyderabad, to conduct the audit of the cost records of the 
Company for the financial year 2026-27, at a remuneration 
of ₹ 1,50,000/- (Rupees One Lakh Fifty Thousand Only) plus 
applicable taxes and out of pocket expenses, at actuals in 
connection with the aforesaid audit.
M/ s. M P R & Associates, Cost Accountants (Firm Reg. No: 
000413), Hyderabad, have furnished a certificate regarding 
their eligibility for appointment as Cost Auditors of the 
Company. They have vast experience in the field of cost audit.
In accordance with the provisions of Section 148 of the 
Companies Act, 2013 read with Rule 14 of the Companies 
(Audit and Auditors) Rules, 2014, the remuneration payable to 
the Cost Auditors as recommended by the Audit Committee 
and approved by the Board of Directors needs to be ratified 
by the members of the Company. Accordingly, consent of the 
members is being sought for ratification of the remuneration 
payable to the Cost Auditors for the financial year 2026-27.
None of the Directors, Key Managerial Personnel of the 
Company and their relatives are in any way deemed to be 
interested or concerned in this Resolution.
The Board recommends the Ordinary Resolution, as set out at 
Item No. 5 of the notice, for approval by the members.
Item No. 6: Appointment of Mr. Jasthi Krishna 
Kishore (DIN: 08001625) as an Independent 
Director of the Company:
Based on the recommendation of the Nomination and 
Remuneration Committee, the Board of Directors at its meeting 
held on July 25, 2026, appointed Mr . Jasthi Krishna Kishore as 
an Additional Director of the Company with effect from July 
25, 2026 pursuant to the provisions of Section 161(1) of the 
Companies Act, 2013 (“the Act”) and Articles of Association of 
the Company. The Board of Directors also appointed Mr . Jasthi 
Krishna Kishore as an Independent Director of the Company not 
liable to retire by rotation, for a period of 3 (three) consecutive 
years commencing from July 25, 2026 upto July 24, 2029 (both 
days inclusive), subject to approval by the Members of the 
Company. 
Brief Profile:
Mr . Jasthi Krishna Kishore, aged 62 years, is a Commerce 
Graduate with Distinction and a qualified Chartered 
Accountant. He joined the Indian Revenue Service in 1990 and 
retired in February 2024 as Chief Commissioner of Income Tax 
after a distinguished career spanning more than three decades 
in government service.
Throughout his illustrious career , he held several senior 
positions in the Government of India and the Government 
of Andhra Pradesh and gained extensive experience in 
taxation, finance, economic development, investment 
promotion, civil aviation, infrastructure development and 
public administration. His notable assignments include Chief 
Commissioner of Income Tax, Karnataka; Principal Director of 
Income Tax (Investigation), Odisha; Principal Commissioner of 
Income Tax, New Delhi; Commissioner of Income Tax, Guntur 
and Vijayawada; and Additional Commissioner of Income Tax, 
Hyderabad and Faridabad.
He also served as Chief Executive Officer of the Andhra 
Pradesh Economic Development Board and Secretary to the 
Government of Andhra Pradesh from 2015 to 2019. During 
his tenure, he played a key role in establishing the Andhra 
Pradesh Economic Development Board and promoting the 
State as a leading investment destination. He was instrumental 
in facilitating investments exceeding ₹2,00,000 crore, 
including investments by prominent companies such as Kia 
Motors, Foxconn and Gamesa. Under his leadership, the Board 
received the SKOCH National Gold Award for Best Investment 
Promotion Agency, and Andhra Pradesh secured the first rank in 
the World Bank’s Ease of Doing Business rankings.
Mr . Jasthi has also held important assignments in the Union 
Ministries of Civil Aviation, Urban Development, Rural 
Development and Agriculture. During his tenure with the 
Ministry of Civil Aviation, he contributed to several significant 
initiatives, including the revival of SpiceJet, restoration of 
flight operations at Visakhapatnam Airport following Cyclone 
Hudhud and the development of major airport projects.
He was awarded the Finance Minister’s Gold Medal for 
Best All-Round Performance in the 45 th Batch of the Indian 
Revenue Service. He has also received several awards and 
commendations from the Central Board of Direct Taxes and 
the Ministry of Finance for excellence in tax administration, 
assessments and infrastructure development. He has served 
on various important government committees, including those 
relating to IRS cadre restructuring, tax accounting standards 
and infrastructure development.
Mr . Jasthi has authored a book on income tax applicable to 
charitable and religious institutions and co-authored the book 
“Towards an Urban Revolution” . He has delivered lectures at 
professional bodies, chambers of commerce and educational 
institutions on taxation, economy, management, leadership and 
motivation. He is also registered as an Arbitrator with the Indian 
Council of Arbitration and is actively engaged in mentoring 
start-ups and educational institutions.
Currently, he serves as an Independent Director on the Boards 
of Taj GVK Hotels & Resorts Limited, Prasaditya ARC Limited, 
Mohan Spintex India Limited , Sri Sarvaraya Sugars Limited 
and Hyderabad Institute of Oncology Private Limited. He 
also advises leading organisations on taxation and general 
management.

evaluating refinancing and capital-structure strategies.

Lakhs during the financial year 2024-25.
DIVIDEND:
In view of the overall performance of the Comapny, while 
retaining capital to support future growth and in line with the 
Dividend Distribution Policy, the Board at its meeting held on 
May 01, 2026, recommended a final dividend of ₹ 1 per equity 
share of ₹ 1 each fully paid (i.e., 100% of the face value), subject 
to the approval of members at the ensuing 33rd Annual General 
Meeting (the “AGM”). The dividend, if approved at the AGM 
will be paid to those members whose names appear on the 
register of members of the Company as of end of the day on 
August 21, 2026 (“Record Date”). The total dividend payout 
will be approximately ₹ 902.90 Lakhs (including tax). In terms 
of the provisions of the Income Tax Act, 1961, dividend income 
is taxable in the hands of the members, and therefore will be 
subject to deduction of applicable tax.
In terms of Regulation 43A of the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India (Listing Obligations and Disclosure 
Requirements) Regulations, 2015 (“SEBI Listing Regulations”), 
the Dividend Distribution Policy, is available on the Company’s 
website at https:/ /www.zentechnologies.com/investor_

Brief details of the performance of the subsidiaries of the 
Company are given below:
Unistring Tech Solutions Private Limited 
Unistring Tech Solutions Private Limited (UTS) is a material 
subsidiary of the Company and is engaged in the business of 
design and development of various products in Electronic 
Warfare (EW), Communication and RADAR applications. UTS 
offers services to Government and private clients in the area 
of EW systems (ESM, COMINT , ELINT & Jammers), RADARs, 
Drone based EW systems, command links, EW and Radar Target 
Simulators.
FINANCIAL STATEMENTSTATUTORY REPORTSSTRATEGIC REVIEW
59

--- page 44 ---
During the year under review, UTS’s revenue from operations 
was ₹ 21,140.35 Lakhs for the year ended March 31, 2026, and 
Profit after tax was ₹ 5,601.45 Lakhs.
Applied Research International Private Limited
Applied Research International Private Limited (ARIPL) is a 
material subsidiary of the Company and is engaged in the 
business of (i) providing simulation and assessment tools for 
the marine, offshore, naval, ports & terminals, construction and 
mining industries; (ii) providing services relating to maintenance, 
certification, assessment solutions with respect to (i) above; (iii) 
fleetview monitoring of ships; and (iv) maintenance and module 
development for e-governance of DG Shipping of India.
During the year under review, ARIPL ’s revenue from operations 
was ₹ 13,116.45 Lakhs for the year ended March 31, 2026, and 
Profit after tax was ₹ 3,968.17 Lakhs.
ARI Labs Private Limited
ARI Labs Private Limited (ALPL) is a wholly owned subsidiary 
of the Company and is engaged in the business of providing 
simulation and assessment tools for the marine and  
naval industries.
During the year under review, ALPL ’s revenue from operations 
was ₹ 6.11 Lakhs for the year ended March 31, 2026, and Loss 
for the year was ₹ 0.40 Lakhs.
Vector Technics Private Limited
Vector Technics Private Limited (Vector), is a subsidiary of the 
Company and engaged in the business of providing propulsion 
and power distribution solutions for drones and UAVs. Its 
current product portfolio includes BLDC motors, electronic 
speed controllers (ESCs), propellers, and starter generators—
key components used in drones, UAVs, and robotic systems.
During the year under review, Vector’s revenue from operations 
was ₹ 924.36 Lakhs for the year ended March 31, 2026, and 
Loss for the year was  ₹ 491.96 Lakhs.
Zen Technologies Inc, USA
Zen Technologies Inc, USA is a wholly-owned subsidiary of 
the Company and is engaged in the business of simulator 
industry, which complements the parent Company’s core 
competencies. Zen Technologies Inc. is primarily dedicated 
to offering combat training products to defense and security 
customers worldwide. It incurred a net loss of USD 6.39 Lakhs 
for the year ended March 31, 2026.
Zen Medical Technologies Private Limited 
Zen Medical Technologies Private Limited (ZMTPL) is a wholly-
owned subsidiary of the Company in India. Zen Medical 
is primarily involved in the field of medical and hospital 
equipment. It incurred a net loss of ₹ 2.19 Lakhs for the year 
ended March 31, 2026.
Zen Defence Technologies L.L.C,
Zen Defence Technologies L.L.C, UAE (ZDT) is a wholly-owned 
subsidiary in Abu Dhabi and is engaged in the business of 
import and export of training equipment and simulators, as well 
as in trading, development, and maintenance of defense and 
surveillance systems on a global scale. It incurred a net loss of 
AED 0.17 Lakhs for the year ended March 31, 2026.

BOARD EVALUATION: 
Under the provisions of the Companies Act, 2013 and 
SEBI Listing Regulations, the Company has carried out the 
Board Evaluation process of the performance of the Board, 
Board Committees, Directors including Executive Directors, 
Independent Directors, and the Chairman. This exercise 
was carried out following the Company’s Nomination and 
Remuneration Policy within the framework of applicable laws. 
The questionnaire and the evaluation process were reviewed in 
line with the SEBI guidance note and suitably aligned with the 
requirements. 
While evaluating the performance and effectiveness of the 
Board, various aspects of the Board’s functioning, such as 
adequacy of the composition and quality of the Board, time 
devoted by the Board to the Company’s long-term strategic 
issues, the quality and transparency of Board discussions, and 
execution and performance of specific duties, obligations, 
and governance were taken into consideration. Committee 
performance was evaluated on their effectiveness in carrying 
out respective mandates, composition, the effectiveness of the 
committees, the structure of the committees and meetings of 
the committee, and its contribution to decisions of the Board. A 
separate exercise was carried out to evaluate the performance 
of Executive Director including the Chairman of the Board and 
Independent Directors, who were evaluated on parameters 
such as level of engagement and contribution to Board 
deliberations, independence of judgement, safeguarding the 
interests of the Company, focus on the creation of shareholder’s 
value, ability to guide the Company in key matters, attendance 
at meetings, etc. The Directors expressed their satisfaction with 
the evaluation process.
NOMINATION AND REMUNERATION POLICY: 
The Board, based on the recommendation of the Nomination 
and Remuneration Committee, framed and adopted the 
Nomination and Remuneration Policy for selection, appointment 
and removal of Directors, Senior Management, Key Managerial 
Personnel (“KMP”) including their remuneration. The 
Committee plays an important role in selection of Directors, 
Senior Management and KMP inter-alia including determination 
of qualifications, experience, expertise, and board diversity.
The Non-Executive Directors are remunerated by way of 
sitting fees for attending Board and Committee meetings. The 
remuneration to a Whole-time Director/Executive Directors 
is broadly divided into fixed and variable components. The 
remuneration payable to them is subject to approval of the 
members of the Company.
For senior management, the remuneration is based on their 
performance, Company’s performance, individual targets 
achieved, industry benchmark and compensation trends. Their 
remuneration consists of monthly salary, bonus, perquisites, KPI 
and other retirement benefits.
The Company’s Nomination and Remuneration Policy is made 
available at https:/ /www.zentechnologies.com/policies-and-
code-of-conduct.
POLICY FOR SELECTION OF DIRECTORS AND 
DETERMINING DIRECTORS’ INDEPENDENCE:
The Nomination and Remuneration Committee (NRC) 
shall assess the independence of directors at the time of 
appointment, re-appointment and the Board shall assess the 
same annually based on the criteria provided by NRC. The 
Board shall re-assess determination of independence when any 
new interests or relationships are disclosed by a Director .

PARTICULARS OF CONTRACTS OR 
ARRANGEMENTS WITH RELATED PARTIES:
The Company has in place a robust process for approval of 
Related Party Transactions (“RPTs”) and dealing with Related 
Parties. The Company demonstrates a structured approach 
to manage RPTs. Transparency and oversight is ensured by 
providing detailed justifications to the Audit Committee and 
adhering to regulatory requirements (SEBI Master Circulars and 
SEBI Listing Regulations).
All related-party transactions (RPT) entered during the financial 
year were conducted in the ordinary course of business and on 
an arms-length basis. The Company, during the year , has not 
entered into any materially significant related-party transactions 
with promoters, Directors, key managerial personnel, or 
other persons that may have had a potential conflict with the 
Company’s interests. All related-party transactions are placed 
before the Audit Committee for review and approval. Prior 
omnibus approval is also obtained from the Audit Committee 
for repetitive related-party transactions that can be foreseen. 
The required disclosures are accordingly made to the Audit 
Committee every quarter regarding their omnibus approval.
Under Regulations 23(5) of SEBI Listing Regulations, as 
amended, approval of the Audit Committee is not applicable 
for the RPTs entered into between a holding Company and its 
wholly-owned subsidiary, and RPT entered into between two 
wholly owned subsidiaries of the listed holding Company, 
whose accounts are consolidated with such a holding 
Company and placed before the shareholders at the general 
meeting for approval. Since most Company transactions are 
with its subsidiaries, omnibus approval of the Audit Committee 
is obtained for such transactions and is reviewed quarterly as a 
measure of good corporate governance.
The policy on the materiality of related-party transactions 
and on dealing with related-party transactions is in line with 
SEBI Listing Regulations, as amended, and is uploaded on the 
website at https:/ /www.zentechnologies.com/policies-and-
code-of-conduct.
In accordance with Section 134(3)(h) of the Companies Act, 
2013 and Rule 8(2) of the Companies (Accounts) Rules, 2014, the 
particulars of the contracts or arrangements with related parties 
referred to in Section 188(1) of the Companies Act, 2013, in  
Form AOC-2 is attached as Annexure III to this Report. 
The Company has obtained approval of the Members for 
continuing/undertaking RPTs which may exceed the materiality 
threshold, and which are in the ordinary course of business 
and on arms’ length. Detailed disclosure on related party 
transactions as per IND AS-24 containing name of the related 
party and details of the transactions entered with such related 
party have been provided under Notes to financial statements. 
Disclosure on RPTs on half year basis are also submitted to the 
stock exchanges i.e BSE Limited and National Stock Exchange 
of India Limited.

updates on regulatory front, industry developments and any 
other significant matters of importance.

Act, 2013 read with Companies (Audit & Auditors’) Rules, 2014

required to be audited.

(Registration No: 000413) to audit the cost records of the 
Company for the FY 2026-27 at a remuneration of ₹ 1,50,000/-

expenses at actuals.  M/ s M P R & Associates have confirmed 
that their appointment is in compliance with the provisions of 
the Companies Act, 2013.

remuneration payable to the Cost Auditors is required to be 
placed before the Members in a general meeting for ratification. 
Accordingly, a resolution seeking Member’s ratification for the 
remuneration payable to M/ s M P R & Associates Cost Auditors 
is included in the Notice convening the AGM.

DETAILS IN RESPECT OF ADEQUACY OF 
INTERNAL FINANCIAL CONTROLS WITH 
REFERENCE TO THE FINANCIAL STATEMENTS:
Your Company has established a comprehensive internal 
control framework and procedures to ensure the efficient 
management of its business operations. Clearly defined roles 
and responsibilities across management support a structured 
governance framework. 
The Audit Committee plays a key role in assessing the 
effectiveness of the internal control framework and 
recommending continuous improvements. The Company 
benchmarks its internal processes against industry standards 
and operates a robust Management Information System to 
support informed decision-making. The Audit Committee 
works closely with the statutory auditors, regularly reviews key 
observations and reports significant findings to the Board of 
Directors. Internal audits are conducted by an independent 
firm of chartered accountants in accordance with an annual 
audit plan approved by the Audit Committee.
The Company has identified key financial reporting risks and 
implemented appropriate control mechanisms to mitigate 
them. These controls are periodically reviewed and updated 
to align with evolving business requirements, regulatory 
developments and technology advancements. Pursuant to 
Section 177 of the Companies Act, 2013 and Regulation 18 of 
the SEBI (LODR) Regulations, 2015, the Audit Committee has 
confirmed that, as of March 31, 2026, the Company’s internal 
financial controls are adequate and operating effectively.

DISCLOSURE UNDER THE SEXUAL 
HARASSMENT OF WOMEN AT WORKPLACE 
(PREVENTION, PROHIBITION AND 
REDRESSAL) ACT , 2013:
Your Company has zero tolerance towards sexual harassment 
at the workplace has adopted a policy on Prevention of Sexual 
Harassment of Women at Workplace in accordance with the 
Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, 
Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013. The Company has taken 
several initiatives across the organization to build awareness 
amongst employees about the Policy and the provisions of 
the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, 
Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013. An Internal Compliance 
Committee (ICC) has been constituted in compliance with 
the requirements of said Act to redress complaints received 
regarding sexual harassment. All employees are covered 
under this Policy.
The details of sexual harassment complaints as per the 
provisions of the Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace 
(Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013, and the Rules 
thereunder are provided in the Coporate Governance Report.

INSIDER TRADING:
The Company adopted a Code of Conduct to Regulate, 
Monitor and Report Trading by Designated Persons and 
Immediate Relatives of Designated Persons pursuant the 
Securities and Exchange Board of India (Prohibition of Insider 
Trading) Regulations, 2015. This Code of Conduct also 
includes code of practices and procedures for fair disclosure 
of unpublished price sensitive information and has been 
made available on the Company’s website at https:/ /www.
zentechnologies.com/policies-and-code-of-conduct.
The Company is maintaining Structured Digital Database 
(‘SDD’), for monitoring the dealings in the securities of the 
Company by the promoters, directors and designated persons 
including immediate relative and also to keep record of the 
persons with whom the unpublished price sensitive information 
of the Company has been shared internally or externally until it 
becomes public.

EDUCATION AND PROTECTION FUND:

and Refund) Rules, 2016, as amended, declared dividends

for a period of 7 years was transferred to the Investor Education 
and Protection Fund by the Company in November , 2025.

6,458 equity shares to the Investor Education and Protection 
Fund relating to the investors who have not claimed any 
dividend from the last seven consecutive years.

shares are transferred are available on the website of the 
Company at https:/ /www.zentechnologies.com/unpaid-

Annexure-V
Annual Report on CSR Activities for the FY 2025-26
(As per Rule 8 of the Companies (Corporate Social Responsibility Policy) Rules, 2014)
1. Brief outline on CSR Policy of the Company:
Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) reflects Zen’s commitment to operating in a manner that balances economic growth with 
social and environmental well-being. Zen is committed conducting its business responsibly, sustainably, and inclusively. The CSR 
Policy articulates the Zen’s approach to contributing meaningfully to the development of communities and addressing pressing 
social and environmental challenges. The Company undertakes CSR initiatives in accordance with Schedule VII of the Companies 
Act, 2013, with a focus on promoting inclusive growth, social equity, and long-term value creation.
This Policy applies to all CSR activities undertaken by the Company, either directly or through implementing partners. It serves as 
a guiding framework to ensure that the Company’s actions go beyond legal compliance and are aligned with the highest ethical 
standards and internationally accepted norms. The goal is to generate a positive and measurable impact on stakeholders including 
the environment, employees, consumers, communities, and society at large by embedding responsibility and accountability into the 
Company’s decision-making and business processes.

To, 
The Members, 
Zen Technologies Limited,
Hyderabad
We have conducted the Secretarial Audit of the compliance 
of applicable statutory provisions and the adherence to 
good corporate practices by Zen Technologies Limited 
(hereinafter referred to as “the Company”). The Secretarial Audit 
was conducted in a manner that provided us a reasonable basis 
for evaluating the corporate conducts/ statutory compliances 
and expressing our opinion thereon. 
Based on our verification of the Company’s books, papers, 
minute books, forms and returns filed and other records 
maintained by the Company and also the information 
provided by the Company, its officers, agents and authorized 
representatives during the conduct of the Secretarial Audit, 
we hereby report that, in our opinion, the company has, during 
the audit period covering the financial year ended March 31, 
2026 complied with the statutory provisions listed hereunder 
and also that the Company has proper board-processes and 
compliance-mechanism in place to the extent, in the manner 
and subject to the reporting made hereinafter: 
We have examined the books, papers, minute books, forms and 
returns filed and other records maintained by the Company for 
the financial year ended March 31, 2026 according to the 
provisions of: 
i. The Companies Act, 2013 (the Act) (applicable sections 
as on date) and the Rules made thereunder; 
ii. The Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 (‘SCRA’) 
and the Rules made thereunder;
iii. The Depositories Act, 1996 and the Regulations and Bye-
laws framed thereunder by the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India (SEBI); 
iv. Foreign Exchange Management Act, 1999 and the Rules 
and Regulations made thereunder to the extent of Foreign 
Direct Investment and Overseas Direct Investment;
v. The following Re gulations and Guidelines prescribed 
under the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 
1992 (‘SEBI Act’):
a. The Se curities and Exchange Board of India 
(Substantial Acquisition of Shares and Takeovers) 
Regulations, 2011; 
b. Securities and Exchange Board of India (Listing 
Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) 
Regulations, 2015;
c. The Se curities and Exchange Board of India 
(Prohibition of Insider Trading) Regulations, 2015; 
d. The Securities and Exchange Board of India (Issue of 
Capital and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 
2018;
e. Securities and Exchange Board of India (Depositories 
and Participants) Regulations, 2018;
f. The Se curities and Exchange Board of India (Share 
Based Employee Benefits and Sweat Equity) 
Regulations, 2021.
vi. Provisions of the following R egulations and Guidelines 
prescribed under the Securities and Exchange Board of 
India Act, 1992 (‘SEBI Act’) were not applicable to the 
Company during the financial year under report:
a. The Se curities and Exchange Board of India 
(Issue and Listing of Non-Convertible Securities) 
Regulations 2021;
b. The Se curities and Exchange Board of India 
(Delisting of Equity Shares) Regulations, 2021; and 
c. The Se curities and Exchange Board of India (Buy-
Back of Securities) Regulations, 2018.

Corporate Affairs. 
During the period under review, the Company has complied 
with the provisions of the Acts, Rules, Regulations, Guidelines, 
Standards, etc. mentioned hereinabove.

Our report of even date is to be read along with this letter .

opinion on these secretarial records based on our audit.

of the contents of the Secretarial records. The verification was done on test basis to ensure that correct facts are reflected in 
secretarial records. We believe that the processes and practices we followed provide a reasonable basis for our opinion.

happening of events etc.
5. The compliance of the provisions of corporate and other applicable laws, rules, regulations and standards is the responsibility 
of the management. Our examination was limited to the verification of procedures on test basis.

with which the management has conducted the affairs of the Company.

Annexure-VI
Statement of particulars of Conservation of Energy, Technology Absorption, Foreign Exchange Earnings and Outgo pursuant to the 
provisions of Section 134(3)(m) of the Companies Act, 2013 read with Rule 8 of Companies (Accounts) Rules, 2014
A. CONSERVATION OF ENERGY 
(i) The steps taken or impact on conservation of energy:
 The Company does not use energy-intensive equipment 
for its operations. Besides, due to significant awareness 
campaigns within the Company, the employees are averse 
to wasting power . Consequently, power consumption 
is one of the lowest per employee. The computers, air-
conditioners and other equipment being used by the 
Company are energy-efficient and environment-friendly.  
(ii) The step s taken by the company for utilizing alternate 
sources of energy: 
a. The Comp any is energy conscious. All types of 
driving simulators manufactured by the Company 
with high-rated, power-saving servo motors.
b. The employee s are disciplined on saving energy. 
Systems are switched on only when it is to be used 
and switched off as soon as the scheduled work is 
completed.
c. The Compan y has made a policy decision of 
buying systems that are rated high in power saving. 
Employees work on LED monitors. Their energy 
consumption is less. They also release less heat 
compared to CRT and LCD monitors enabling the 
centralized air-conditioner maintain the temperature 
with ease. The air-conditioner too has a regulator to 
save power .
d. Plans are afoot to buy eco-friendly vehicles for intra-
office movement at the Hardware Park Plant and 
between the research wing and production wings.
e. Also ther e is a move to harness solar energy for 
lighting and wire fencing. The roof of the plant is 
about 70 feet from ground and there are enough 
provisions for the day light to seep into the plant to 
enable technical hands to work without switching on 
electrical lights especially in day time.
f. Air conditioners are fit ted with controllers to cut off  
power at the set temperature. The present MH lamps 
and mercury lamps are replaced with LED lamps 
which consume only 40% of CFL and its minimum 
life is 50,000 burning hours.
g. The str eet lights and the complete indoor lights 
at the Hardware Park are replaced with LED bulbs 
towards efficient energy consumption.
h. The compan y has installed motion sensor based 
lights in the corporate & head office and the 
Hardware Park.
i. We had also installed energy meters to our 40 Kva & 
60 Kva UPS for monitoring Electric consumption for 
our IT infrastructure.
(iii) The capit al investment on energy conservation 
equipments: The capital investment was made on 
controllers used for air conditioners and LED bulbs.
B. TECHNOLOGY ABSORPTION
(i) The efforts made towards technology absorption:
 The Compan y has indigenously developed significant 
technologies that are useful in various products. The 
technologies nurtured within the Company have been 
incorporated into various products.
(ii) The benefits derive d like product improvement, cost 
reduction, product development or import substitution:
 We expect such technologies will give us an unbeatable 
edge in evolving our products into advanced, reliable, 
and robust simulators.
(iii) In case of imported technology (imported during the last 
three years reckoned from the beginning of the financial 
year): 
 The Compan y has not imported any technology during 
the past 3 years.
(iv) The expenditure incurred on Research and Development:
 The Compan y has incurred ₹ 3,689.60 Lakhs as R&D 
expenditure (capital and revenue) for the financial year 
2025-26 for the development of various products. The 
Company has incurred 8.70% as R&D expenditure of 
Sales Turnover . The Company will continue to make big 
bets for long-term national interests which may impact 
short-term profitability of the Company.
